9.5.1. Applications
   (1) The above discussion is important to cover the space of local forms which are not allowed to
depend explicitly on the xI. Indeed, in that space HN(d) is represented for p(n by the constant
forms c  2 N dxI 2dxIN (Theorem 4.3). Now, the equations of motion may be such that some of
        I I
the constant forms become trivial in H(s"d). We know that this cannot happen in form-degree zero,
but nothing prevents it from happening in higher form-degrees. For instance, for a single abelian
gauge "eld with Lagrangian ¸"(!1/4)FIJF #kIA with constant kI, the equations of
                                                        IJ       I
motion read R FJI#kI"0 and imply that the constant (n!1)-form 夹k"
                      J
(1/(n!1)!)dxI 2dxIL\ e  2 L kIL is trivial in H(s"d), s夹AH#d夹F#(!)L夹k"0. Hence, for this
                            I I
Lagrangian (9.14) is not ful"lled. Of course, the example is academic and the Lagrangian is not
Lorentz-invariant. The triviality of 夹k in H(s"d) is a consequence of the linear term in the
Lagrangian.
   Eq. (9.14) is ful"lled in the space of x-independent local forms for Lagrangians having no linear
part in the "elds, for which the equations of motion reduce identically to 0"0 when the "elds are
set to zero. It is also ful"lled if one restricts one's attention to the space of PoincareH invariant local
508                        G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569

forms. (And it is also trivially ful"lled in the space of all local forms with a possible explicit
x-dependence, as we have seen.) For this reason, (9.14) does not appear to be a drastic restriction in
the space of x-independent local forms.
   Note that the classi"cation of the elements (and the number of these elements) in a basis of H(s)
having the properties of Theorem 9.1 depend on the context. For instance, for a single abelian
gauge "eld with ghost C, dxIC is non-trivial in the algebra of local forms with no explicit
x-dependence, and so can be taken as a h ; but it becomes trivial if one allows for an explicit
                                             G
x-dependence, dxIC"d(xIC)#s(xIA), and so can be regarded in that case as a hK  .
                                                                                        G
   (2) Another instance where the descent equations with the above assumption on H(d) play a role
is the cohomology H(d"d, I ), where I is the space of G-invariant local forms depending only on
                            Q            Q
the variables sSD de"ned in Section 8.1 and on the xI and dxI. HN(d, I ) is represented for p(n by
                                                                         Q
the `characteristic classesa, i.e., by G-invariant polynomials P(F) in the curvature 2-forms
F' [62,101] (these polynomials are d-exact in the space of all local forms, but they are not d-exact in
I ). Using this result on HN(d, I ), one proves straightforwardly by means of the descent equations
   Q                              Q
that H(d"d, I ) is isomorphic to the characteristic cohomology in the space of gauge invariant local
              Q
forms (`equivariant characteristic cohomologya) which will play an important role in the analysis
of the consistency condition performed in Section 11.
   (3) Finally, we mention that the above discussion was used within the computation of the local
BRST cohomology in Einstein}Yang}Mills theory [25]. In that case, HN(d) is non-trivial in certain
form-degrees p(n due to the non-trivial De Rham cohomology of the manifold in which the
vielbein "elds take their values. The corresponding aNN ful"ll (9.14) and have a non-trivial descent
                                                       G
(in contrast, the constant forms and the characteristic classes met in the two instances discussed
before do not descend).

10. Cohomology in the small algebra

10.1. Dexnition of small algebra

  The `small algebraa B is by de"nition the algebra of polynomials in the undi!erentiated ghosts
C', the gauge "eld 1-forms A' and their exterior derivatives dC' and dA':
      B"+polynomials in C', A', dC', dA', .
It is stable under d and s (b3BNdb3B, sb3B). This is obvious for d and holds for s owing to
       sC'"ef ' C)C(, sA'"!dC'!ef ' C(A) ,
              ()                              ()
       sdC'"!ef ' C(dC), sdA'"ef ' (C)dA(!A(dC)) .                                                  (10.1)
                   ()                    ()
Accordingly, the cohomological groups H(s"d, B) of s modulo d in B are well de"ned.
   The small algebra B is only a very small subspace of the complete space of all local forms (in fact
B is "nite dimensional whereas the space of all local forms is in"nite-dimensional). Nevertheless it
provides a good deal of the BRST cohomology in Yang}Mills theories, in that it contains all the
anti"eld-independent solutions of the consistency condition sa#db"0 that descend non-trivially,
in a sense to be made precise in Section 11. Furthermore, it will also be proved there that the
representatives of H(s"d, B) remain non-trivial in the full cohomology, with only very few possible
exceptions.
   For this reason, the calculation of H(s"d, B) is an essential part of the calculation of H(s"d) in the
full algebra. This calculation was done "rst in [96,97] (in fact in the universal algebra de"ned
below).
   Let us brie#y outline the construction of H(s"d, B) before going into the details. It is based on an
analysis of the descent equations described in Section 9. The central task in this approach is the



   Note that s and c coincide in the small algebra, which contains no anti"eld.
512                          G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569

explicit construction of a particular basis of H(s, B) as in Theorem 9.1 which provides H(s"d, B) by
Theorem 9.2. Technically, it is of great help that the essential steps of this construction can be
carried out in a free di!erential algebra associated with B. This is shown "rst.

10.2. Universal algebra

  The free di!erential algebra associated with B is denoted by A. It was called the `universal
algebraa in [96,97]. It has the same set of generators as B, but these are not constrained by the
condition coming from the space}time dimension that there is no exterior form of form-degree
higher than n.
  Explicitly, A is generated by anticommuting variables C'A , A'A and commuting variables (dC)'A ,
(dA)'A which correspond to C', A', dC' and dA' respectively, A is the space of polynomials in these
variables,

      A"+polynomials in C'A , A'A , (dC)'A , (dA)'A , .

These variables are subject only to the commutation/anticommutation relations C'A C(A "
!C(A C'A , etc., but are not constrained by the further condition that the forms are zero whenever
their form-degree exceeds n. Thus, the free di!erential algebra A is independent of the space}time
dimension; hence its name `universala.
   The fundamental di!erence between A and B is that the A'A , (dC)'A and (dA)'A are variables by
themselves, whereas their counterparts A', dC' and dA' are composite objects containing the
di!erentials dxI and jet-space variables ("elds and their derivatives), A'"dxIA' , dC'"dxIR C',
                                                                                  I            I
dA'"dxIdxJR A' .
               I J
   The universal algebra A is in"nite-dimensional, whereas B is "nite-dimensional since the
space}time dimension bounds the form-degree of elements in B. By contrast, A contains elements
with arbitrarily high degree in the (dC)'A and (dA)'A .
   The usefulness of A for the computations in the small algebra rests on the fact that A and B are
isomorphic at all form-degrees smaller than or equal to the space}time dimension n. To make this
statement precise, we "rst de"ne a `p-degreea which is the form-degree in A,

                 R              R                R
      p"A'A          #(dC)'A         #2(dA)'A         ,
                RA'A         R(dC)'A          R(dA)'A

and clearly coincides with the form-degree in B for the corresponding objects. The p-degree is
indicated by a superscript. A and B decompose into subspaces with de"nite p-degrees,

although the anomaly itself is meaningful both in A and B.

11. General solution of the consistency condition in Yang}Mills-type theories

11.1. Assumptions

  We shall now put the pieces together and determine H(s"d, X) completely in Yang}Mills-type
theories for two cases:
      case I: X"+all local forms, ,
      case II: X"+PoincareH -invariant local forms, ,                                             (11.1)
on the following assumptions:
                             G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569                     525

   (a) the gauge group does not contain abelian gauge symmetries under which all matter "elds are
uncharged } we shall call such special abelian symmetries `free abelian gauge symmetriesa in the
following;
   (b) the space}time dimension (denoted by n) is larger than 2;
   (c) the theory is normal and the regularity conditions hold;
   (d) in case II it is assumed that the Lagrangian itself is PoincareH -invariant (in case I it need not be
PoincareH -invariant).
   Assumption (c) is a technical one and has been explained in Sections 5 and 6. Assumptions (a)
and (b) re#ect special properties of free abelian gauge symmetries and two-dimensional (pure)
Yang}Mills theory which complicate somewhat the general analysis. These special properties of
free abelian gauge symmetries are illustrated and dealt with in Section 13 where we compute the
cohomology for a set of free abelian gauge "elds. Two-dimensional pure Yang}Mills theory is
treated separately in the appendix to this section. Note that assumption (a) does not exclude
abelian gauge symmetries. It excludes only the presence of free abelian gauge "elds, or of abelian
gauge "elds that couple exclusively non-minimally to matter or gauge "elds (i.e., through the "eld
strengths and their derivatives only).
   The space of all local forms is the direct product PX(1L) where P is the space of local functions
of the "elds, anti"elds, and all their derivatives, while X(1L) is the space of ordinary di!erential
forms u(x, dx) in 1L. Depending on the context and Lagrangian, P can be, for instance, the space of
polynomials in the "elds, anti"elds, and all their derivatives (when the Lagrangian is polynomial
too), or it can be the space of local forms that depend polynomially on the derivatives of the "elds
and anti"elds but may depend smoothly on (some of) the undi!erentiated "elds (when the
Lagrangian has the same property). The latter would be the case, for instance, for Yang}Mills
theory coupled to a dilaton. It can also be the space of formal power series in the "elds, anti"elds,
and all their derivatives, with coe$cients that depend on the coupling constants (in the case of
e!ective theories). More generally speaking, the results and their derivation apply whenever the
various cohomological results derived and discussed in the previous sections (especially those on
H(s) and H (d)) hold, since these will be used within the computation below.
              
   The space of PoincareH -invariant local forms is a subspace of PX(1L). It contains only those
local forms which do not depend explicitly on the space}time coordinates xI and are Lorentz-
invariant. Lorentz-invariance requires here simply that all Lorentz indices (including the indices of
derivatives and di!erentials, and the spinor indices of space}time fermions) are contracted in an
SO(1, n!1)-invariant manner.
   Two central ingredients in our computation of H(s"d) are the results on the characteristic
cohomology of d in Section 6 and on the cohomology of s in Section 8. We shall repeat them here,
for cases I and II, and reformulate the result on H(s) since we shall frequently use it in that
formulation. For the characteristic cohomology of d we have:

when free abelian gauge symmetries are present, and is not exhausted by the constants in
two-dimensional pure Yang}Mills theory; this makes these two cases special). The analysis and
results of Section 6 extend to case II because both d and d are Lorentz invariant according to the
de"nition of Lorentz invariance used here (i.e., d and d commute in X with SO(1, n!1)-rotations of
all Lorentz indices). This is obvious for d and holds for d thanks to assumption (d) because that
assumption guarantees the Lorentz-covariance of the equations of motion.

11.2. Outline of the derivation and result

  We shall determine the general solution of the consistency condition

      suN#duN\"0, uN, uN\3X                                                                    (11.7)

for all values of the form-degree p and of the ghost number (the ghost number will not be made
explicit throughout this section, contrary to the form-degree). Since the precise formulation and
derivation of the result are involved, we shall "rst outline the crucial steps of the computation and
describe the various non-trivial solutions. The precise formulation of the result and its proof will be
given in the following section.
  The computation relies on the descent equation technique described in Section 9, which can be
used because d has trivial cohomology at all form-degrees di!erent from 0 and n,

      HN(d, X)"dN 1 for p(n .                                                                    (11.8)
                
This holds in the space of all local forms (case I) by the algebraic PoincareH lemma (Theorem 4.2). It
also holds in the space of PoincareH invariant local forms because d is Lorentz-invariant (cf. text
after Corollary 11.1). In fact, one may even deduce this directly from the proof of the algebraic
PoincareH lemma given in Section 4 because the operators o and P used there are manifestly
                                                                         K
Lorentz-invariant, and because there are no Lorentz-invariant constant forms c  2 N dxI 2dxIN
                                                                                    I I
with form degree 0(p(n. In fact, that proof of the algebraic PoincareH lemma is not just an
existence proof but provides an explicit construction of uN\ for given d-closed uN such that
uN"duN\, both in cases I and II.
   One distinguishes between solutions with a trivial descent and solutions with a non-trivial
descent.

11.2.1. Solutions with a trivial descent
  These are solutions to (11.7) that can be rede"ned by the addition of trivial solutions such that
they solve suN"0. The result on H(s) (Corollary 11.2) implies then that these solutions have the
form

      uN"IN ?H , IN ?3I                                                                          (11.9)
              ?
modulo trivial solutions. We note that (11.9) can be trivial in H(s"d) even if IN? . 0.
528                              G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569

11.2.2. Lifts and equivariant characteristic cohomology
   The solutions with a non-trivial descent are those for which it is impossible to make uN\ vanish
in (11.7) through the addition of trivial solutions. Their determination is more di$cult. In
particular, it calls for the solution of a cohomological problem that we have not discussed so far.
Namely, one has to determine the characteristic cohomology in the space I de"ned in (11.6). This
cohomology is well de"ned because dILI. Indeed, for I3I, one has dI"DI, where D"dxID ,
                                                                                                      I
with D the covariant derivative on the "elds and D xJ"dJ . Furthermore DI is G-invariant. We
        I                                                I      I
call this cohomology the `equivariant characteristic cohomologya and denote it by H (d, I). It is
                                                                                           
related to, but di!erent from the ordinary characteristic cohomology discussed in Section 6.
   To understand the di!erence, assume that I3I is weakly d-exact in X, i.e., I+du for some
u3X. The equivariant characteristic cohomology poses the following question: is it possible to
choose u3I? We shall answer this question in the a$rmative with the exception when I contains
a `characteristic classa. Abusing slightly standard terminology, a characteristic class is in this
context a G-invariant polynomial in the curvature 2-forms F' and is thus, in particular, an element
of the small algebra. Furthermore, we shall show that no characteristic class with form-degree
(n is trivial in H (d, I) (at form-degree n there may be exceptions).
                       
   Hence, H (d, I) is the sum of a subspace of H (d, X) (given by H (d, X)5I) and of the
                                                                           
space of characteristic classes. Using Corollary 11.1, one thus gets that H (d, I) is at all
                                                                                      
form-degrees (n!1 solely represented by characteristic classes (at form-degree n!2 this is due
to assumptions (a) and (b)). In contrast, there are in general additional non-trivial representatives of
H (d, I) at form-degrees n and n!1. At form-degree n they are present because an n-form is
   
automatically d-closed but not necessarily weakly d-exact. The additional representatives at
form-degree n!1 are gauge-invariant non-trivial Noether currents written as (n!1)-forms.
   The result on H (d, I) is interesting in itself and a cornerstone of the local BRST cohomology
                      
in Yang}Mills-type theories. The technical assumption of `normalitya assumed throughout the
calculation is made in order to be able to characterize completely H (d, I). The properties of
                                                                            
H (d, I) are at the origin of the importance of the small algebra for the cohomology, as we now
   
explain.
   The equivariant characteristic cohomology arises as follows when discussing the descent equa-
tions. One has

11.3. Main result and its proof

   According to the discussion in Section 11.2 it may appear natural to determine "rst H (d, I)
                                                                                             
and afterwards H(s"d, X). In fact that strategy was followed in previous computations [23,24].
However, it is more e$cient to determine H (d, I) and H(s"d, X) at a stroke. The reason is that
                                              
these two cohomologies are strongly interweaved. In fact, not only does one need H (d, I) to
                                                                                         
compute H(s"d, X); but also, H (d, I) at form-degree p can be computed by means of H(s"d, X) at
                               
lower former degrees using descent equation techniques. That makes it possible to determine both
cohomologies simultaneously in a recursive manner, starting at form-degree 0 where H(s"d, X)
reduces to H(s, X), and then proceeding successively to higher form-degrees. This strategy stream-
lines the derivation as compared to previously used approaches (but reaches of course identical
conclusions!), and is re#ected in the formulation and proof of the theorem given below. The
theorem is formulated such that it applies both to cases I and II; however, the di!erent meaning of
X, I (and thus also jD and <D ) in these cases should be kept in mind.
                               ?
   To formulate and prove the theorem, we use the same notation as in Section 11.2. In addition
we introduce, similarly to (11.16), the notation MN, NN and bN for linear combinations of the
                               G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569                        533

[M  2 )  2 , ]Q , N  2 )  2 , (h(C), f (F)), and [M  2 )  2 , ]Q >KP  with form-degree p, respective-
    P P Q Q          P PQ Q                            P P Q Q
ly,

      MN,      jP 2P) Q 2Q, M  2 )  2 , (h(C), f (F)) ,                                             (11.34)

11.4. Appendix. Two-dimensional pure Yang}Mills theory

   Pure two-dimensional Yang}Mills theory needs a special treatment because HL\(d, X),
                                                                                      

   
symmetries (Theorem 6.8), but is much bigger and in fact in"nite-dimensional (see explicit
description at the end of the appendix). This feature disappears if one couples coloured matter
"elds. We discuss the pure Yang-Mills case for the sake of completeness contenting ourselves with
case I, i.e., with the solution of the consistency condition suN#duN\"0 in the space of all local
forms.

12. Discussion of the results for Yang}Mills-type theories

  Theorem 11.1 gives the general solution of the consistency condition sa#db"0 at all form-
degrees and ghost numbers for theories of the Yang}Mills type without free abelian gauge sym-
metries (in the sense of Section 11.1) and in space}time dimensions greater than 2. The case of free
abelian symmetries is treated in the next section. In this section, we spell out the physical
implications of the theorem by expliciting the results in the relevant ghost numbers.
  To that end we shall use the notation f ([F, t] ) for functions that depend only on the
                                                       "
Yang}Mills "eld strengths, the matter "elds and their covariant derivatives,

      f ([F,t] ),f (F' , D F' , D D F' ,2,tG, D tG, D D tG,2) .
              "      IJ M IJ M N IJ            I     I J
  We recall that the results are valid for general Lagrangians of the Yang}Mills type, provided
these ful"ll the technicality assumptions of `regularitya and `normalitya explained above. The
results cover in particular the standard model and e!ective gauge theories.

12.1. H\ L(s"d): global symmetries and Noether currents

12.1.1. Solutions of the consistency condition at negative ghost number
  We start with the discussion of the results at negative ghost number. First, we recall that the
groups H\O L(s"d) are trivial for q'1. This implies that there is no characteristic cohomology in
form degree (n!1, i.e., no non-trivial higher order conservation law. Any conserved local
antisymmetric tensor AI 2IO (q'1) is trivial, i.e., equal on-shell to the divergence of a local
antisymmetric tensor with one more index,

      q'1: R  AI 2IO +0, AI 2IO "A I 2IO NAI 2IO +R  BI I 2IO , BI I 2IO "B I I 2IO .
            I                                           I
We stress again that the important point in this statement is that the BI I 2IO are local functions;
the statement would otherwise be somewhat empty due to the ordinary PoincareH lemma for 1L.
  The only non-vanishing group at negative ghost number is H\ L(s"d, X). The non-trivial
representatives of H\ L(s"d, X) are the generators of the non-trivial global symmetries, denoted by
KD and K in the previous section. Indeed, one must set H "1 in order that Eqs. (11.21) and
                                                               ?
(11.33) yield solutions with ghost number !1. The general solution of the consistency condition
with ghost number !1 is thus

      u\ L&jDKD #jK ,

12.1.3. Examples

1. It should be noted that the gauge-covariant form of a global symmetry is not always its most
   familiar version. In order to make a global symmetry gauge covariant, it may be necessary to
   add a trivial symmetry to it. We illustrate this feature now for conformal transformations.


   To our knowledge, it is still an open problem whether in standard Yang}Mills theory characteristic classes P(F) with
form-degree n can be trivial in H (d, I). (The problem occurs only in the space of forms with explict xI-dependence.) In
                                    

the assertion given there is incorrect because the sl(n)-decomposition of the equations of motion used there does not yield
pieces which are all weakly zero separately. If the answer were positive (contrary to our expectations), it would mean that
non covariantizable currents could occur in standard Yang}Mills theory, contrary to the claim in Theorem 2 in [22].

which case the consistency condition implies absence of anomalies, for any Lagrangian.

13.3. Uniqueness of Yang}Mills cubic vertex

  We now use the above results to discuss the consistent deformations of the action (13.1).
Requiring that the interactions be PoincareH invariant, the relevant results are those of case II.
  As shown in [19], the consistent deformations of an action are given, to "rst order in the
deformation parameter, by the elements of H L(s"d), i.e., here, from (13.20),

      u L"[夹CH'R #(夹AH')A(R R #(夹F')A(A)R R R ]P(C)
                 '          ( '           ) ( '
             # BL#IL#[KD #jD A'R ]PD(C) ,                                                     (13.22)
                                '
where P(C) has ghost number 3 and PD(C) ghost number one (P (C) in (13.20) has ghost number
                                                                 ?
zero and thus is a constant; this has been taken into account in (13.22)).
  The term BL is the familiar Chern}Simons term [80], and exists only in odd dimensions. It
belongs to the small algebra and is of the form AF2F. The term IL is strictly gauge-invariant and
thus involves the abelian "eld strengths and their derivatives. Born}Infeld or Euler}Heisenberg
deformations are of this type. Since these terms are well understood and do not a!ect the gauge
symmetry, we shall drop them from now on and focus on the other two terms, which are

      [夹CH'R #(夹AH')A(R R #(夹F')A(A)R R R ]P(C)                                              (13.23)
            '          ( '           ) ( '
and

      [KD #jD A'R ]PD(C) .                                                                    (13.24)
                 '
Expression (13.23) involves the anti"elds conjugate to the ghosts, while (13.24) involves only the
anti"elds conjugate to A'.
556                         G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569

  Now, it has also been shown in [19] (see [136] for further details) that deformations involving
non-trivially the anti"elds do deform the gauge symmetries. Those that involve the anti"elds
conjugate to the ghosts deform not only the gauge transformations but also their algebra; while
those that involve only AH modify the gauge transformations but leave the gauge algebra
                            '
unchanged (at least to "rst order in the deformation parameter).
  Writing P(C)"(1/3!) f C'C(C) (with f          completely antisymmetric), one gets from (13.23)
                         '()                '()
that the deformations of the theory that deform the gauge algebra are given by

      夹CH'f C(C)#(夹AH')A(f C)#(夹F')A(A)f
           '()            '()           '()
         " !dLx( f C)C(CH'#f A( C)AHI'# f FIJ'A( A)) .                                           (13.25)
                 '()        '() I       '()    I J
The term independent of the anti"elds is the "rst-order deformation of the action, and one
recognizes the standard Yang}Mills cubic vertex } except that the f           are not subject to the Jacobi
                                                                          '()
identity at this stage. This condition arises, however, when one investigates consistency of the
deformation to second order: the deformation is obstructed at second order by a non-trivial
element of H L(s"d) unless          (f f       #f     f   #f       f    )"0 [20]. The obstruction is
                                   ) '() )*+       (*) )'+     *') )(+
precisely of the type (13.23), with P(C)"!(1/36) f f                  C'C(C*C+. The "eld monomials in
                                                         ) '() )*+
front of the anti"elds in (13.25) give the deformations of the BRST transformations of the ghosts
and gauge "elds, respectively (up to a minus sign) and provide thus the non-abelian extension of the
abelian gauge transformations and their algebra. Thus, one recovers the known fact that the
Yang}Mills construction provides the only deformation of the action for a set of free abelian gauge
"elds that deforms the algebra of the gauge transformations at "rst order in the deformation
parameter. Any consistent interaction which deforms non-trivially the gauge algebra at "rst order
contains therefore the Yang}Mills vertex. Furthermore, this deformation automatically incorpor-
ates the Lie algebra structure underlying the Yang}Mills theory, without having to postulate it
a priori. This result has been derived recently in [219], along di!erent lines and under stronger
assumptions on the form of the new gauge symmetries. These extra assumptions are in fact not
necessary as the cohomological derivation shows.
   Having dealt with the deformations (13.23), we can turn to the deformations (13.24), which do
not deform the (abelian) gauge algebra at "rst order although they do deform the gauge trans-
formations. These involve Lorentz covariant and gauge invariant conserved currents jID . An
example of a deformation of this type is given by the Freedman}Townsend vertex in three
dimensions [111,3]. In four dimensions, however, the results of [202] indicate that there is no
(non-trivial) candidate for jID . There is an in"nite number of conservation laws because the theory is
free, but these do not involve gauge-invariant Lorentz vectors. Thus, there is no PoincareH invariant
deformation of the type (13.24) in four dimensions. This strengthens the above result on the
uniqueness of the Yang}Mills cubic vertex, which is the only vertex deforming the gauge trans-
formations in four dimensions. Accordingly, in four dimensions, the most general deformation of
the action for a set of free abelian gauge "elds is given, at "rst order, by the Yang}Mills cubic vertex
and by strictly gauge invariant deformations. We do not know whether the results of [202]
generalize to higher dimensions, leaving the (unlikely in our opinion) possibility of the existence of
interactions of the type (13.24) in n'4 dimensions, which would deform the gauge transformations
without modifying the gauge algebra at "rst order in the deformation parameter.
                            G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569                557

14. Three-dimensional Chern}Simons theory

14.1. Introduction } H(s)

  We shall now describe the local BRST cohomology in three-dimensional pure Chern}Simons
theory with general gauge group G, i.e., G may be abelian, semisimple, or the direct product of an
abelian and a semisimple part. Since pure Chern}Simons theory is of the Yang}Mills type,
Theorem 11.1 applies to it when the gauge group is semisimple. So, the Chern}Simons case is not
really special from this point of view. However, the results are particularly simple in this case
because the theory is topological. As we shall make it explicit below, there is no non-trivial local,
gauge-invariant function and the BRST cohomology reduces to the Lie algebra cohomology with
coe$cients in the trivial representation.
  It is because of this, and because of the physical interest of the Chern}Simons theory, that we
devote a special section to it.
  The Chern}Simons action is [80]

Eqs. (14.3) and (14.4) provide the solutions of the consistency condition with a trivial descent. They
also yield the bottom forms which can appear in non-trivial descents. To "nd all solutions with
a non-trivial descent, we investigate how far these bottom forms can be lifted to solutions with
higher form-degree.

14.3. BRST cohomology in the case of PoincareH -invariant forms

   This case is easy. By Eq. (14.4), all non-trivial bottom forms that can appear in case II are either

15. References for other gauge theories

   In this section, we give references to works where the previous algebraic techniques have been
used to "nd the general solution of the consistency condition with anti"elds included (and for the
BRST di!erential associated with gauge symmetries) in other "eld theoretical contexts.
   Some aspects of local BRST cohomology for the Stueckelberg model are investigated in [95].
The general solution of the Wess}Zumino consistency condition for gauged non-linear p-models is
discussed in [140,142]. Cohomological techniques (PoincareH lemma, BRST cohomology) have
been recently analyzed on the lattice in [172,112,195]. Algebraic aspects of gravitational anomalies
[2] are discussed in [18,38,165,57,186,93]. The general solution of the consistency condition
without anti"elds is given in [64]; this work is extended to include the anti"elds in space}time
dimensions strictly greater than two in [25], where again, the cohomology of the Koszul-Tate
di!erential is found to play a crucial role.
   In 2 space}time dimensions, these groups have been analyzed in the context of the (bosonic)
string world sheet action coupled to backgrounds in [42,44,49,225,15,16,73,197,53]. Complete
results, with the anti"elds included, are derived in [67,68,70]. Algebraic results on the Weyl
anomaly [74,79,81] may be found in [56,58]. Algebraic aspects of the consistency condition for
N"1 supergravity in four dimensions have been discussed in [59,43,66,182]. The complete
treatment, with anti"elds included, is given in [69]. For p-form gauge theories, the local BRST
cohomology groups without anti"elds have been investigated in [39] and more recently, with
anti"elds, in [137}139,212,113,141,159].

Remark

  To follow the rules of Physics Reports, we have singled out some papers. Our selection criteria
have been
562                            G. Barnich et al. / Physics Reports 338 (2000) 439}569

E papers marked by *** are at the root of the algebraic approach to renormalization of gauge
  theories,
E papers marked by ** contain results on the BRST cohomology that initiated and/or developed
  techniques that we have extensively used; we have also included here papers that provide strong
  motivations for studying this problem,
E papers marked by * develop important aspects of the algebraic approach to gauge theories and
  the anti"eld formalism but lie somewhat out of the main line of our report.

  We have included in the classi"cation neither our work nor mathematical papers that we have
used, because both are explicitly quoted in the text.
